Let's assess the position more thoroughly.

White's pieces: King g1, Rook f1, Bishop e3, Pawns a2, c2, c3, f2, g2, h2.

White's bishop is on e3, a good central square. Black's rooks are on a8 and f6. Black's king is on c8, exposed. White's king is on g1, relatively safe. The main imbalance: white has bishop + pawns vs two rooks. Usually, two rooks are stronger than rook + bishop, but white has three extra pawns, so material is balanced. Positionally, white's pawns are a bit scattered: doubled c-pawns, missing d and e pawns, but the pawns on a2, c2, c3, f2, g2, h2 form a solid enough structure. Black has only three pawns, so white has a big majority on the kingside (3 vs 1) and queenside (3 vs 2). White can create a passed pawn.

I think the best plan for white is to advance the kingside pawns, gain space, and perhaps create an attack on black's king or win more material. But we must also keep an eye on black's rook activity.
